in this subsection. For the sake of brevity, we won’t state all possible commutations for every equation, but restrict ourselves to the ones we actually need within the bootstrap argument. We also refer to Appendix
11.2
for expressions for commutators of spatial differential operators with each other and with
\partial_{t}
.

The terms written down explicity in Lemma
2.11
are ones that dominate the evolution behaviour or that are the largest higher order terms, both of which require careful treatment within the bootstrap argument. The error terms are broadly categorized into three groups:

•

“Borderline”
terms are terms that critically contribute to the fact that the energies diverge toward the Big Bang singularity. This almost always takes the form of adding energy terms at the same order as the evolved variable scaled by factors of the type
\varepsilon a^{-3}
or
\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
, which causes the energies to slightly diverge since
a^{-3}
is barely not integrable (see (
2.6
)).

•

“Junk”
terms are terms that are subcritical in the sense that they lead to integrable error terms, or terms that only contain lower order derivatives of the solution variables.

•

“Top”
order terms (which only appear in (
2.38a
) and (
2.38b
)) are terms that are terms that are junk terms for low order energies, but become borderline terms at top order.

All of these error terms are tracked schematically in Section
11.3
. Since we will only need
L^{2}_{G}
-bounds on these error terms, which are given in Section
11.4
, we will treat them as notational
“black boxes”
outside of the appendix.

Lemma 2.11

(Laplace-commuted rescaled equations)
.

Let
L\in 2\mathbb{N},\,L\geq 2
. With error terms as defined in Appendix
11.3
, the system in Proposition
2.10
leads to the following Laplace-commuted equations:

The
Laplace-commuted rescaled evolution equations for the second fundamental form

(2.35)

\partial_{t}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma=-a\nabla^{2}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N+a(N+1)\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\text{{Ric}}[G]+\mathfrak{S}_{L,Border}+\mathfrak{S}_{L,Junk}\,,

the
Laplace-commuted rescaled momentum constraint equations

(2.36a)

\text{{div}}_{G}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma=-8\pi(\Psi+C)\left[\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi+\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}-1}\text{{Ric}}[G]\ast\nabla\phi\right]+\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}-1}\text{{Ric}}[G]\ast\Sigma+\mathfrak{M}_{L,Junk}

and

(2.36b)

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{{curl}}_{G}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma=-a^{-2}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{B}+\bm{\varepsilon}[G]\ast\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}-1}\text{{Ric}}[G]\ast\Sigma+\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{L,Junk}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,,

the
Laplace-commuted rescaled Hamiltonian constraint equations

(2.36c)

\displaystyle\,\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}R[G]+a^{-4}\sum_{I_{1}+I_{2}=L}\nabla^{I_{1}}\Sigma\ast\nabla^{I_{2}}\Sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,16\pi Ca^{-4}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi+a^{-4}\sum_{I_{1}+I_{2}=L}\left[\nabla^{I_{1}}\Psi\ast\nabla^{I_{2}}\Psi+\nabla^{I_{1}+1}\phi\ast\nabla^{I_{2}+1}\phi\right]

and

(2.36d)

\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\text{{Ric}}[G]=a^{-4}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{E}-\frac{\tau}{3}a^{-1}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma+\mathfrak{H}_{L,Border}+\mathfrak{H}_{L,Junk}\,,

the
Laplace-commuted rescaled lapse equations

(2.37a)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}N=

\displaystyle\,\left(12\pi C^{2}a^{-4}+\frac{1}{3}\right)\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N+16\pi Ca^{-4}\cdot\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi+\mathfrak{N}_{L,Border}+\mathfrak{N}_{L,Junk}\,,

(2.37b)

\displaystyle\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}N=

\displaystyle\,\left(12\pi C^{2}a^{-4}+\frac{1}{3}\right)\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N+16\pi Ca^{-4}\cdot\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi+\mathfrak{N}_{L+1,Border}+\mathfrak{N}_{L+1,Junk}\,,

the
Laplace-commuted rescaled Bel-Robinson evolution equations

(2.38a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{E}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\left(3-N\right)\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+(N+1)a^{-1}\text{{curl}}_{G}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{B}-a^{-1}\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N\wedge_{G}\bm{B}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\,+4\pi C^{2}a^{-3}(N+1)\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+4\pi a(\Psi+C)\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N\otimes\nabla\phi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\,+4\pi a(\Psi+C)(N+1)\nabla^{2}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi-8\pi a(N+1)\left(\nabla\phi\otimes\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi\right)

\displaystyle\,+\mathfrak{E}_{L,Border}+\mathfrak{E}_{L,top}+\mathfrak{E}_{L,Junk}

(2.38b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{B}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\dot{a}}{a}(3-N)\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{B}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-(N+1)a^{-1}\text{{curl}}_{G}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\bm{E}+a^{-1}\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N\wedge_{G}\bm{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\,+a^{3}\bm{\varepsilon}[G]\ast\nabla^{2}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi\ast\nabla\phi+\mathfrak{B}_{L,Border}+\mathfrak{B}_{L,top}+\mathfrak{B}_{L,Junk}\,,

the
Laplace-commuted rescaled matter evolution equations

(2.39a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi=

\displaystyle\,a\langle\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N,\nabla\phi\rangle_{G}+a(N+1)\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}\phi-3C\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N+\mathfrak{P}_{L,Border}+\mathfrak{P}_{L,Junk}

(2.39b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi=

\displaystyle\,a^{-3}(N+1)\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi+Ca^{-3}\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N+\mathfrak{Q}_{L,Border}+\mathfrak{Q}_{L,Junk}

as well as (also allowing
L=0
for (
2.39d
))

(2.39c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nabla_{l}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi=

\displaystyle\,a\nabla_{l}\nabla^{\sharp j}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N\nabla_{j}\phi+a(N+1)\nabla_{l}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}\phi-3C\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\nabla_{l}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N

\displaystyle\,+\left(\mathfrak{P}_{L+1,Border}\right)_{l}+\left(\mathfrak{P}_{L+1,Junk}\right)_{l}

(2.39d)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}\phi=

\displaystyle\,a^{-3}(N+1)\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}\Psi+Ca^{-3}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}N+\mathfrak{Q}_{L+1,Border}+\mathfrak{Q}_{L+1,Junk}

and the
Laplace-commuted rescaled Ricci evolution equations

(2.40a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\text{{Ric}}[G]_{ij}=

\displaystyle\,a^{-3}\left(\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}\Sigma_{ij}-2\nabla^{\sharp m}\nabla_{(i}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma_{j)m}\right)

\displaystyle\,-\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N+\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}N\cdot G_{ij}\right)+\left(\mathfrak{R}_{L,Border}\right)_{ij}+\left(\mathfrak{R}_{L,Junk}\right)_{ij}

(2.40b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nabla_{k}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\text{{Ric}}[G]_{ij}=

\displaystyle\,a^{-3}\left(\nabla_{k}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}\Sigma_{ij}-2\nabla_{k}\nabla^{\sharp m}\nabla_{(i}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma_{j)m}\right)

\displaystyle\,-\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\left(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N+\nabla_{k}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}+1}N\cdot G_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle\,+\left(\mathfrak{R}_{L+1,Border}\right)_{ijk}+\left(\mathfrak{R}_{L+1,Junk}\right)_{ijk}\,.

Proof.

The equations (
2.36c
),(
2.36d
) and (
2.37a
) are obtained by simply applying
\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}
on both sides of (
2.29a
),(
2.29c
) and (
2.30a
) respectively. For (
2.36a
) and (
2.36b
), we additionally use the commutator formulas (
11.7e
) and (
11.7f
), while for the evolution equations, we apply the respective commutator of
\partial_{t}
and spatial derivatives as collected in Lemma
11.7
and commute Laplacians past
\nabla
and
curl
where needed (see the commutators in Lemma
11.5
). The commutators with
\partial_{t}
only cause additional borderline and junk terms that don’t substantially influence the behaviour, while the spatial commutators often lead to high order curvature terms, for example the Ricci terms in (
2.36a
), that need to be more carefully tracked.
∎

Remark 2.12

(Simplified junk term notation)
.

For junk terms that occur in an inner product with a tracefree symmetric tensor, any terms that are pure trace will immediately cancel and thus don’t need to be taken into consideration for the following estimates, even if they have to be written down in the junk terms. Hence, we will denote with a superscript
“
\parallel
”
(for example
\mathfrak{H}_{L,Junk}^{\parallel}
) on a schematic error term the expressions that arise when dropping all terms of the form
\zeta\cdot G
for some scalar function
\zeta
that occur in this term’s definition (see, for example, (
11.11d
)).

3.
Big Bang stability: Norms, energies and bootstrap assumptions

Herein, we state the norms and energies we use to control the solution variables. These will allow us to state our initial data and bootstrap assumptions, and we then provide which improvement we aim to achieve for the latter. Note that we don’t provide the coerciveness of our energies immediately (and actually cannot, at least not in a manner useful to our analysis), but will establish Sobolev norm control in the proof of Corollary
7.3
, the key ingredient being Lemma
4.5
. Furthermore, we collect a local well-posedness statement from previous work in Section
3.4
.

3.1.
Norms

Recall that
\gamma
is the hyperbolic spatial reference metric on
M
introduced in Definition
2.1
, which we view as a metric on any foliation hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
(see Section
1.2.1
), and
G
is the rescaled spatial metric arising from the evolution (see Definition
2.9
).

Definition 3.1

(Pointwise norms and volume forms)
.

We denote by
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\gamma}
(resp.
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{G(t,\cdot)}
) the pointwise norm with regards to
\gamma
(resp.
G(t,\cdot)
). For the sake of simplicity, we define
\lvert\zeta\rvert_{\gamma}=\lvert\zeta\rvert_{G(t,\cdot)}=\lvert\zeta(t,\cdot)\rvert
for any scalar function
\zeta
on
\Sigma_{t}
. The volume forms on
\Sigma_{t}
with respect to
\gamma
and
G(t,\cdot)
are written as
{\text{{vol}}}_{\gamma}
and
{\text{{vol}}}_{G(t,\cdot)}
(or just
{\text{{vol}}}_{G}
).

Definition 3.2

(
L^{2}
-norms)
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent
(r,s)
-tensor field (for
r,s\geq 0
). Then, we define:

(3.1a)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}

\displaystyle\,:=\int_{M}\lvert\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\rvert_{\gamma}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{\gamma},

(3.1b)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}

\displaystyle\,:=\int_{M}\lvert\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\rvert_{G(t,\cdot)}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G(t,\cdot)}\,

Definition 3.3

(Sobolev norms)
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be as above and
J\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We define:

(3.2a)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{J}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{J}_{\gamma}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\,\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\left\lvert\hat{\nabla}^{J}\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\right\rvert_{\gamma}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{\gamma}

(3.2b)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{J}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{J}_{G}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\,\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\left\lvert\nabla^{J}\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\right\rvert_{G(t,\cdot)}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G(t,\cdot)}

(3.2c)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{H^{J}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{J}_{\gamma}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{k=0}^{J}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{k}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}

(3.2d)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{H^{J}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{J}_{G}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{k=0}^{J}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{k}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}

Definition 3.4

(Supremum norms)
.

For
\mathfrak{T}
as above and
J\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we set:

(3.3a)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{\gamma}}=

\displaystyle\,\sup_{p\in\Sigma_{t}}\left\lvert\hat{\nabla}^{J}\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\right\rvert_{\gamma},

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{J}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}=\sum_{k=0}^{J}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{C}^{k}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t})}

(3.3b)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}=\|\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{G}}=

\displaystyle\,\sup_{p\in\Sigma_{t}}\left\lvert\nabla^{J}\mathfrak{T}(t,\cdot)\right\rvert_{G(t,\cdot)},

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{J}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}=\sum_{k=0}^{J}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{C}^{k}_{G}(\Sigma_{t})}

Remark 3.5

(Time dependence is suppressed in notation)
.

When the choice of
t
and
\Sigma_{t}
is clear from context, we will often drop time dependences of
G
,
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{G}
,
{\text{{vol}}}_{G}
and
\mathfrak{T}
, surpress the hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
in the Sobolev and supremum norms, and simply write
\int_{M}
instead of
\int_{\Sigma_{t}}
. For example, we write

\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}=\int_{M}\lvert\mathfrak{T}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\,.

Definition 3.6

(Solution norms)
.

We define the following norms to measure the size of near-FLRW solutions:

(3.4a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=

\displaystyle\,\|\Psi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}H^{18}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}+\|\nabla\phi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}H^{17}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}+a^{2}\|\nabla\phi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\dot{H}^{18}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\,+\|\Sigma\|_{H^{18}_{G}}+\|\bm{E}\|_{H^{18}_{G}}+\|\bm{B}\|_{H^{18}_{G}}

\displaystyle\,+\|G-\gamma\|_{H^{18}_{G}}+\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\|_{H^{16}_{G}}+a^{-4}\|N\|_{H^{16}_{G}}+a^{-2}\|N\|_{\dot{H}^{17}_{G}}+\|N\|_{\dot{H}^{18}_{G}}

(3.4b)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{H}_{top}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a^{2}\|\Psi\|_{{\dot{H}^{19}_{G}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+a^{4}\|\nabla\phi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\dot{H}^{19}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+a^{2}\|\Sigma\|_{\dot{H}^{19}_{G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+a^{2}\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\|_{\dot{H}^{17}_{G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,

(3.4c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=

\displaystyle\,\|\Psi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{16}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}+\|\nabla\phi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{15}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}+\|\Sigma\|_{C^{16}_{G}}+\|\bm{E}\|_{C^{16}_{G}}+\|\bm{B}\|_{C^{16}_{G}}

\displaystyle\,+\|G-\gamma\|_{C^{16}_{G}}+\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\|_{C^{14}_{G}}+a^{-4}\|N\|_{C^{14}_{G}}+a^{-2}\|N\|_{\dot{C}^{15}_{G}}+\|N\|_{\dot{C}^{16}_{G}}

(3.4d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}=

\displaystyle\,\|\Psi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{16}_{\gamma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}+\|\nabla\phi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{15}_{\gamma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}+\|\Sigma\|_{C^{16}_{\gamma}}+\|\bm{E}\|_{C^{16}_{\gamma}}+\|\bm{B}\|_{C^{16}_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\,+\|G-\gamma\|_{C^{16}_{\gamma}}+\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\|_{C^{14}_{\gamma}}+a^{-4}\|N\|_{C^{14}_{\gamma}}+a^{-2}\|N\|_{\dot{C}^{15}_{\gamma}}+\|N\|_{\dot{C}^{16}_{\gamma}}

Remark 3.7

(Choice of metric and controlling Christoffel symbols)
.

We could equivalently also phrase
\mathcal{H}
in terms of
\gamma
-norms, or predominately use
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}
instead of
\mathcal{C}
, since we include the norms on
G-\gamma
and
\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G=(\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}\gamma)+\frac{2}{9}(G-\gamma)
. We will demonstrate in Lemma
7.4
how
H_{G}
and
C_{\gamma}
norms can be used to control
H_{\gamma}
and
C_{G}
norms. We will also indicate how the initial data and bootstrap assumptions for
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{C}
are equivalent in Remarks
3.11
and
3.18
. The main reason for this is that, by successively replacing local coordinates in the expressions of
\Gamma-\hat{\Gamma}
by
\hat{\nabla}
, one has

(3.5)

\|\Gamma-\hat{\Gamma}\|_{C^{l-1}_{\gamma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\lesssim P_{l}(\|G-\gamma\|_{C^{l}_{\gamma}(M)},\|G^{-1}-\gamma^{-1}\|_{C^{l}_{\gamma}(M)})\,.

We choose to work predominately with norms in terms of the rescaled metric since quantities appearing in the Einstein system are naturally contracted by
G
, not
\gamma
, and we commute with differential operators associated with
G
.

Remark 3.8

(Redundancies in the solution norms)
.

The solution norms
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}
aren’t
“optimal”
in the sense that controlling the norms of
\Psi,\,\nabla\phi,\Sigma
and
G-\gamma
is entirely sufficient to gain the claimed control (up to constant) on
N
via the lapse equation,
\bm{E}
and
\bm{B}
via to the constraint equations and
\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G
via local coordinates. We choose to include all variables in the norms and subsequent assumptions mainly for the sake of convenience.

3.2.
Energies

The fundamental objects used to control the solution variables are the energies that take the following form:

Definition 3.9

(Energies)
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We define:

(3.6a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(l)}(\phi,t)=

\displaystyle\,(-1)^{l}\int_{M}\Psi\Delta^{l}\Psi-a^{4}\phi\Delta^{l+1}\phi\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\int_{M}\lvert\Delta^{\frac{l}{2}}\Psi\rvert^{2}+a^{4}\lvert\nabla\Delta^{\frac{l}{2}}\phi\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}&l\text{ even}\\
\int_{M}\lvert\nabla\Delta^{\frac{l-1}{2}}\Psi\rvert_{G}^{2}+a^{4}\lvert\Delta^{\frac{l+1}{2}}\phi\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}&l\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(3.6b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(l)}(W,t)=

\displaystyle\,(-1)^{l}\int_{M}\langle\bm{E},\Delta^{l}\bm{E}\rangle_{G}+\langle\bm{B},\Delta^{l}\bm{B}\rangle_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

(3.6c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(l)}(\Sigma,t)=

\displaystyle\,(-1)^{l}\int_{M}\langle\Sigma,\Delta^{l}\Sigma\rangle_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

(3.6d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(l)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)=

\displaystyle\,(-1)^{l}\int_{M}\left\langle\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G,\Delta^{l}\left(\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\right)\right\rangle_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

(3.6e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(l)}(N,\cdot)=

\displaystyle\,(-1)^{l}\int_{M}\langle N,\Delta^{l}N\rangle_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

Usually, we will use integration by parts to distribute derivatives within the energies as in (
3.6
). Further, we introduce the notation

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\leq l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{l}\mathcal{E}^{(i)}

for any of the energies above.

For any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and any smooth functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we have

(3.8)

f_{1}\cdot f_{2}\cdot\mathcal{E}^{(2l+1)}\leq\frac{f_{1}^{2}}{2}\mathcal{E}^{(2l)}+\frac{f_{2}^{2}}{2}\mathcal{E}^{(2l+2)}\,.

Performing the calculation for
\Sigma
as an example, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(2l+1)}(\Sigma,\cdot)

\displaystyle\,=-\int_{M}\langle\Delta^{l}\Sigma,\Delta^{l+1}\Sigma\rangle_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\leq\int_{M}\lvert\Delta^{l}\Sigma\rvert_{G}\lvert\Delta^{l+1}\Sigma\rvert_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{E}^{(2l)}(\Sigma,\cdot)}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}^{(2l+2)}(\Sigma,\cdot)}

Now, (
3.8
) then follows from the Young inequality. As a consequence, we also have

(3.9)

\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l)}\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{l}\mathcal{E}^{(2m)}\,,\,\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l+1)}\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{l+1}\mathcal{E}^{(2m)}\,.

This allows us to largely restrict our analysis to even order energies, outside of how we close the bootstrap argument at top order.

3.3.
Assumptions on the initial data

With the necessary solution norms and energies now defined, we can now state what we assume near-FLRW initial data to satisfy:

Assumption 3.10

(Near-FLRW initial data)
.

For some small enough
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and the solution norms
\mathcal{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\mathcal{H}_{top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\mathcal{C}
as in Definition
3.6
, we assume the rescaled inital data to be close to that of the FLRW solution in Lemma
2.3
in the following sense:

(3.10)

\mathcal{H}(t_{0})+\mathcal{H}_{top}(t_{0})+\mathcal{C}(t_{0})\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}

The assumptions on
\mathcal{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\mathcal{H}_{top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
also imply the following:

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}18\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}(\phi,t_{0})+\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 18)}(\Sigma,t_{0})+\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 18)}(W,t_{0})+\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 16)}(\text{{Ric}},t_{0})

\displaystyle+\|\nabla\phi\|_{H^{18}_{G}}^{2}+\mathcal{E}^{(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}18\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}(N,t_{0})+a(t_{0})^{-4}\mathcal{E}^{(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}17\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}(N,t_{0})+a(t_{0})^{-8}\mathcal{E}^{(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq 16\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}(N,t_{0})

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+a(t_{0})^{4}\mathcal{E}^{(19)}(\phi,t_{0})+a(t_{0})^{4}\mathcal{E}^{(19)}(\Sigma,t_{0})+a(t_{0})^{4}\mathcal{E}^{(17)}(\text{{Ric}},t_{0})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}

Remark 3.11

(Initial data size in
C_{\gamma}(t_{0})
)
.

Notice that by (
3.5
), (
3.10
) implies that

(3.12a)

\|\Gamma-\hat{\Gamma}\|_{C^{15}_{G}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{t_{0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}\,,

and arguing along similar lines and using
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
-estimates, also

(3.12b)

\|\Gamma-\hat{\Gamma}\|_{H^{17}_{G}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{t_{0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}\,.

In particular, since moving from
C_{\gamma}^{l}
to
C_{G}^{l}
only requires control on Christoffel symbols to order
l-1
for general tensors and
l-2
for scalar functions, as well as zero order control on
G-\gamma
, it follows from (
3.10
) that

(3.13)

C_{\gamma}(t_{0})\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}

We refer to the proof of Lemma
7.4
for a more detailed term analysis and how a similar argument also applies to the Sobolev norms.

Remark 3.12

(Redundancies in the initial data assumptions)
.

Similar to Remark
3.8
, one could also reduce the initial data assumptions in (
3.10
), especially at top order. In particular, we highlight that the Bel-Robinson energy can be entirely controlled by the other terms that occur due to the additional scaled
\Sigma
-energy at order
19
, or vice versa we could drop the latter in favour of the former. This will be reflected in Lemma
6.10
.

Remark 3.13

(The volume form)
.

Let
\mu_{G}
and
\mu_{\gamma}
denote the volume elements of
G
and
\gamma
respectively. Since the determinant is a smooth map on invertible matrices, the initial data assumptions on
G-\gamma
also imply

(3.14)

\|\mu_{G}-\mu_{\gamma}\|_{C^{0}_{G}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})}=\|\mu_{G}-\mu_{\gamma}\|_{C^{0}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\,.

Consequently, we have

\|{\text{{vol}}}_{G}-{\text{{vol}}}_{\gamma}\|_{C^{0}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})}=\mu_{\gamma}^{-1}\|{\text{{vol}}}_{\gamma}\|_{C^{0}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})}\|\mu_{G(t_{0},\cdot)}-\mu_{\gamma}\|_{C^{0}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}

and, since
\|G^{-1}-\gamma^{-1}\|_{C^{0}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
also follows by a von Neumann series argument from the initial data assumption on
G-\gamma
,

(3.15)

\|{\text{{vol}}}_{G}-{\text{{vol}}}_{\gamma}\|_{C^{0}_{G}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\,.

3.4.
Local well-posedness and continuation criteria

For everything that follows, we need to establish that the initial data assumptions above also ensure local well-posedness. For the core system, we translate the local well-posedness result for stiff fluids in
[
RS18b
]
to the sub-case of the scalar field system. While statement and proof there are for vanishing spatial sectional curvature and what corresponds to choosing
C=\sqrt{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}
, the arguments for our setting are completely analogous.

Lemma 3.14

(Local well-posedness and continuation criteria for the Einstein scalar-field system (Big Bang version), see
[
RS18b
, Theorem 14.1]
)
.

Let
N\geq 4
be an integer and
(M,\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\pi},\mathring{\psi})
be geometric initial data to the Einstein scalar-field system (see Section
1.2.1
) and assume that one has

\|\mathring{g}-a(t_{0})^{2}\gamma\|_{H^{N+1}_{\gamma}(M)}+\|\mathring{k}+\frac{\tau(t_{0})}{3}\cdot a(t_{0})^{2}\gamma\|_{H^{N}_{\gamma}(M)}+\|\mathring{\pi}\|_{H^{N}_{\gamma}(M)}+\|\mathring{\psi}-Ca(t_{0})^{-3}\|_{H^{N}_{\gamma}(M)}<\infty\,

as well as, for some sufficiently small
\eta^{\prime}>0
,

\|\mathring{\psi}-Ca(t_{0})^{-3}\|_{C^{0}_{\gamma}(M)}\leq\eta^{\prime}\,.

Then, the CMC-transported Einstein scalar-field system (respectively the rescaled system) is locally well-posed in the following sense: The initial data
(\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\pi},\mathring{\psi})
launches a unique classical solution
(g,k,n,\nabla\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
to (
2.16a
)-(
2.16b
), (
2.15a
)-(
2.15b
), (
2.18
) and (
2.17a
) on
[t_{1},t_{0}]\times M
for some
t_{1}\in(0,t_{0})
that satisfies
{k^{l}}_{l}=-3\frac{\dot{a}}{a}
and
n>0
, launches a solution to the Einstein scalar-field system and such that the variables enjoy the following regularity:

\displaystyle g\in

\displaystyle C^{N-1}_{dt^{2}+\gamma}([t_{1},t_{0}]\times M)\cap C^{0}([t_{1},t_{0}],H_{\gamma}^{N+1}(M))

\displaystyle k\in

\displaystyle C^{N-2}_{dt^{2}+\gamma}([t_{1},t_{0}]\times M)\cap C^{0}([t_{1},t_{0}],H_{\gamma}^{N}(M))

\displaystyle\nabla\phi\in

\displaystyle C^{N-2}_{dt^{2}+\gamma}([t_{1},t_{0}]\times M)\cap C^{0}([t_{1},t_{0}],H_{\gamma}^{N}(M))

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi\in

\displaystyle C^{N-2}_{dt^{2}+\gamma}([t_{1},t_{0}]\times M)\cap C^{0}([t_{1},t_{0}],H_{\gamma}^{N}(M))

\displaystyle n\in

\displaystyle\,C^{N}_{dt^{2}+\gamma}([t_{1},t_{0}]\times M)\cap C^{0}([t_{1},t_{0}],H_{\gamma}^{N+2}(M))

The rescaled variables
(G,\Sigma,N,\nabla\phi,\Psi)
enjoy the analogous regularity. If
(\mathfrak{t},t_{0}]
is the maximal interval on which the above statements hold, then one either has
\mathfrak{t}=0
or one of the following blow-up criteria are satisfied:

(1)

The smallest eigenvalue of
g(t_{m},\cdot)
converges to 0 for some sequence
(t_{m},x_{m})\subseteq(\mathfrak{t},t_{0}]\times M
with
t_{m}\downarrow\mathfrak{t}
.

(2)

n(t_{m},x_{m})
converges to
0
for some sequence
(t_{m},x_{m})\subseteq(\mathfrak{t},t_{0}]\times M
with
t_{m}\downarrow\mathfrak{t}
.

(3)

\left(\lvert\partial_{0}\phi\rvert^{2}+\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert_{g}^{2}\right)(t_{m},x_{m})
converges to
0
for some sequence
(t_{m},x_{m})\subseteq(\mathfrak{t},t_{0}]\times M
with
t_{m}\downarrow\mathfrak{t}
.

(4)

s\in(\mathfrak{t},t_{0}]\mapsto\|g\|_{C^{2}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{s})}+\|k\|_{C^{1}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{s})}+\|n\|_{C^{2}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{s})}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{C^{1}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{s})}+\|\nabla\phi\|_{C^{1}_{\gamma}(\Sigma_{s})}
is unbounded.

A note on the proof.

Note that the additional initial data requirement in the stiff-fluid setting that the pressure is strictly positive is covered by the smallness assumption on
\mathring{\psi}-Ca(t_{0})^{2}
, since the pressure corresponds to
\lvert\mathring{\psi}\rvert^{2}+\lvert\mathring{\pi}\rvert_{\mathring{g}}^{2}
and the assumptions on
\partial_{t}\phi
and
\nabla\phi
ensure that (after embedding) this quantity behaves like
C^{2}a(t_{0})^{-6}+\mathcal{O}\left(\eta^{\prime}\right)
at
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.15

(Local well-posedness for the Bel-Robinson variables)
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
3.14
, the Bel-Robinson variables
E
and
B
corresponding to the Lorentzian metric
\overline{g}=-n^{2}dt^{2}+g
satisfy the equations (
2.24a
)-(
2.24b
), are the unique classical solutions to the evolution equations (
2.25a
)-(
2.25b
), and satisfy

E,B\in C^{N-3}([t_{1},t_{0}]\times M)\cap C([t_{1},t_{0}],H^{N-1}_{\gamma}(M))

Proof.

That
E
and
B
satisfy the constraint equations, solve the evolution equations and have the stated regularity on the interval of existence is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.14
and the computations in Section
2.4
. Furthermore, with initial data derived from the constraint equations as in Remark
2.8
, the hyperbolic system (
2.24a
)-(
2.24b
) launches a unique solution satisfying the regularity above that must then be
(E,B)
.
∎

For sufficiently regular initial data (
N\geq 21
), it follows that

\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 19)}(\phi,\cdot),\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 18)}(W,\cdot),\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 19)}(\Sigma,\cdot),\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 17)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot),\|G-\gamma\|_{H^{18}_{G}}\in C^{1}([t_{1},t_{0}])\,,

and similarly the square of any supremum norm occuring in
\mathcal{C}
is continuously differentiable on
[t_{1},t_{0}]
. Strictly speaking, we would need to assume this additional regularity on our initial data for the computations in the following sections (especially Section
6
) to hold. However, since smooth functions are dense in
H^{l}(M)
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, any bounds on
\mathcal{H}(t)
and
\mathcal{C}(t)
that we prove assuming sufficient regularity at
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
then immediately extend to data only satisfying the regularity implied by (
3.10
).

Thus, from here on out, we will assume without loss of genearlity that all energies and squared norms are continuously differentiable on the domain of existence, and similarly all variables are continuously differentiable for the lower order
C_{G}
-norm improvements in Section
4.2
.

3.5.
Bootstrap assumption

To keep an overview of the entire bootstrap argument, we state all of the assumptions and comprehensively list how we intend to improve them.

Assumption 3.16

(Bootstrap assumption)
.

Fix some
t_{Boot}\in[0,t_{0})
. Further, let
c_{0}>0
, let
\sigma\in(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}},1]
be suitably small such that
c_{0}\sigma<1
, and
K_{0}>0
a suitable constant. For any
t\in(t_{Boot},t_{0}]
, we assume

(3.16)

\mathcal{C}(t)\leq K_{0}\varepsilon a(t)^{-c_{0}\sigma}\,.

Remark 3.17
.

More explicitly, (
3.16
) means

(3.17a)

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{16}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17b)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\phi\|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{15}_{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17c)

\displaystyle\|\Sigma\|_{C^{16}_{G}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17d)

\displaystyle\|\bm{E}\|_{C^{16}_{G}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17e)

\displaystyle\|\bm{B}\|_{C^{16}_{G}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17f)

\displaystyle\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\|_{C^{14}_{G}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17g)

\displaystyle\|G-\gamma\|_{C^{16}_{G}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17h)

\displaystyle\|N\|_{C^{14}_{G}}+a^{2}\|N\|_{\dot{C}^{15}_{G}}+a^{4}\|N\|_{\dot{C}^{16}_{G}}\leq

\displaystyle\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{4-c_{0}\sigma}

(3.17i)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|\Gamma-\hat{\Gamma}\|_{C^{15}_{G}}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,K_{0}\varepsilon a^{-c_{0}\sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Remark 3.18

(Bootstrap assumptions with respect to
\gamma
)
.

Note again that we could equivalently make the above bootstrap assumptions with respect to
H_{\gamma}
- and
C_{\gamma}
-norms: For example, the assumptions (
3.17i
) and (
3.17g
) imply

\displaystyle\|\zeta\|_{C^{l}_{\gamma}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,a^{-c\sigma}\|\zeta\|_{C^{l}_{G}}+\|\zeta\|_{C^{\lceil\frac{l-1}{2}\rceil}_{\gamma}}\varepsilon a^{-c\sigma},\quad\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{l}_{\gamma}}\lesssim a^{-c\sigma}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{l}_{G}}+\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{\lceil\frac{l}{2}\rceil}_{\gamma}}\varepsilon a^{-c\sigma}

for any smooth function
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})
, any
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent tensor
\mathfrak{T}
and a constant
c>0
. This is essentially a direct consequence of (
3.5
), and we will prove an improved version of this rigorously in Lemma
7.4
. Applying this to each norm in
\mathcal{C}
, we get

(3.18)

\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-c\sigma}

for some updated constant
c\geq c_{0}
.

Remark 3.19

(Strategy for the bootstrap improvement)
.

Our goal is to improve the
C
-norm estimate to

\mathcal{C}\leq K_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{9}{8}}a^{-c_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}\,,

where
c_{1},K_{1}>0
are positive constants independent of
\sigma
and
\varepsilon
. Notice how this is actually an improvement if we choose
\sigma
suitably and then choose
\varepsilon
sufficiently small: Any update between
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
can be balanced out since we gain at least the additional prefactor
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}
in each estimate, which we can then choose to have been suitably small. Similarly, we improve the power of
a
if we have
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}\cdot\sigma^{-1}<\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}
. If we then retroactively choose
\sigma
large enough compared to
\varepsilon
but small overall – for example
\sigma=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{16}}
– and then ensure that
\max\{c_{0},c_{1}\}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{16}}<1
as well as
c_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{16}}<c_{0}
are satisfied by choosing
\varepsilon
to have been small enough, we have strictly improved the bootstrap assumptions.

Remark 3.20

(Conventions within the bootstrap argument)
.

Throughout the rest of the argument, we tacitly assume
t\in(t_{Boot},t_{0}]
if not stated otherwise, and we assume
\varepsilon
and
\sigma
to be sufficiently small. In the proof of Theorem
8.2
, we will choose
\sigma=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{16}}
, but this explicit choice will not be used or needed up to that point. Finally, we allow
c\geq c_{0}
be a constant that we may update from line to line, and will similarly deal with prefactors by
“
\lesssim
”
-notation where the constant may change in each line. These updates will always be independent of
\sigma
and
\varepsilon
, but may depend on
t_{0}
, and the quantities arising from the FLRW reference solution. Hence, we not only assume
c_{0}\sigma<1
, but
c\sigma<1
throughout the argument.

4.
Big Bang stability: A priori estimates

In this section, we collect strong low order
C_{G}
-norm estimates that follow as an immediate consequence from the bootstrap assumptions, starting with key estimates at the base level and followed by weaker, but still improved estimates at higher levels. Finally, we collect a differentiation formula for integrals with respect to
{\text{{vol}}}_{G}
as well as a Sobolev estimate that lays the groundwork for energy coercivity. In particular, using the strong
C_{G}
-norm estimates, said estimate proves that moving between energies and norms at most an error involving lower order energies of the controlled variable and curvature energies, scaled by
a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
.

4.1.
Strong
C^{0}_{G}
-estimates

First, we establish a pointwise bound on the lapse that actually holds irrespective of the bootstrap assumptions:

Lemma 4.1

(Maximum principle for the lapse)
.

The lapse remains positive and bounded throughout the evolution:

(4.1)

n=N+1\in(0,3]

Proof.

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
be arbitrary and let
n_{min}
be the minimum of
n
over
\Sigma_{t}
at
(t,x_{min})
. Then,
(\Delta_{g}n)(t,x_{min})>0
holds. If
n_{min}
were nonpositive, (
2.17a
) would lead to the following contradiction:

0\geq-12\pi C^{2}a^{-6}-\frac{1}{3}a^{-2}+n_{min}\left[\frac{1}{3}a^{-2}+4\pi C^{2}a^{-6}+\langle\hat{k},\hat{k}\rangle_{g}+8\pi\lvert\partial_{0}\phi\rvert^{2}\right]=\Delta_{g}n(t,x_{min})>0

This shows
n>0
, and the upper bound follows analogously.
∎

The following estimate will be essential in dealing with borderline terms throughout the bootstrap argument:

Lemma 4.2

(Strong
C^{0}_{G}
estimates)
.

The following estimates hold:

(4.2a)

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon

(4.2b)

\displaystyle\|\Sigma\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon

(4.2c)

\displaystyle\|\bm{E}\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon

Proof.

(
4.2a
):
From (
2.32a
), we obtain the following using Lemma
4.1
for
n
, the bootstrap assumptions (
3.17h
) and (
3.17b
) and that
\dot{a}\simeq a^{2}
by (
2.3
):

\displaystyle\lvert\partial_{t}\Psi\rvert\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon a^{5-c\sigma}+\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}+\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}\lvert\Psi\rvert+{\varepsilon}a^{1-c\sigma}

After integration, we thus obtain using the initial data assumption (
3.10
):

\displaystyle\lvert\Psi(t)\rvert\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\lvert\Psi(t_{0})\rvert+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon a(s)^{1-c\sigma}\,ds+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}{\varepsilon}a(s)^{1-c\sigma}\lvert\Psi(s)\rvert\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon\left(1+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}a(s)^{1-c\sigma}\,ds\right)+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}{\varepsilon}a(s)^{1-c\sigma}\lvert\Psi(s)\rvert\,ds

By (
2.6
), the integral over
a^{1-c\sigma}
is bounded since
c\sigma<1
, so the Gronwall lemma now yields (
4.2a
).

(
4.2b
):
Notice that

(4.3)

\partial_{t}\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}={(\partial_{t}\Sigma^{\sharp})^{l}}_{m}{(\Sigma^{\sharp})^{m}}_{l}+{(\Sigma^{\sharp})^{l}}_{m}{(\partial_{t}\Sigma^{\sharp})^{m}}_{l}=2{(\partial_{t}\Sigma^{\sharp})^{l}}_{m}{(\Sigma^{\sharp})^{m}}_{l}\leq 2\lvert\partial_{t}\Sigma^{\sharp}\rvert_{G}\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}\,.

Now, we consider (
2.28d
) and, using the bootstrap assumptions (
3.17h
), (
3.17f
) and (
3.17b
), get:

\displaystyle\lvert\partial_{t}\Sigma^{\sharp}\rvert_{G}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\tau\lvert N\rvert\lvert\Sigma^{\sharp}\rvert_{G}+\lvert\nabla^{\sharp}\nabla N\rvert_{G}a+\lvert N+1\rvert a\left\lvert\text{{Ric}}[G]^{\sharp}+\frac{2}{9}G^{\sharp}\right\rvert_{G}+\lvert N+1\rvert a\lvert\nabla^{\sharp}\phi\nabla\phi\rvert_{G}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{3}\lvert N\rvert\cdot\left(4\pi C^{2}a^{-3}+\frac{1}{9}a\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,{\varepsilon}a^{1-c\sigma}\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}+{\varepsilon}a^{1-c\sigma}

We can now apply Lemma
11.2
with
f=\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}
, and thus have along with (
3.10
) and (
4.3
):

\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}(t)\leq\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}(t_{0})+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\lvert\partial_{t}\Sigma^{\sharp}\rvert_{G}(s)\,ds\lesssim\varepsilon

(
4.2c
):
Using the constraint equation (
2.29c
) and that
\langle G,\bm{E}\rangle_{G}=\text{tr}_{G}\bm{E}=0
, one sees

\lvert\bm{E}\rvert_{G}^{2}=\left\langle a^{4}\left(\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\right)-\dot{a}a^{2}\Sigma-\Sigma\odot_{G}\Sigma-4\pi a^{4}\nabla\phi\nabla\phi,\bm{E}\right\rangle_{G}\,.

Then, applying the bootstrap assumptions (
3.17f
) and (
3.17b
) shows the Ricci and matter terms are bounded by
\varepsilon a^{4-c\sigma}\lvert\bm{E}\rvert_{G}
, and the a priori estimate (
4.2b
) along with
\dot{a}a^{2}\simeq 1
by (
2.3
) bounds the remaining terms by
\varepsilon\lvert\bm{E}\rvert_{G}
. The statement then follows by dividing by
\lvert\bm{E}\rvert_{G}
and taking the supremum.
∎

Note that, in the proof of (
4.2b
), it was essential that we used
(
2.28d
) instead of (
2.28c
), since using the latter would incur terms of the type
\lvert\partial_{t}G\rvert_{G}\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}
and
a^{-3}\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{3}
when computing the time derivative of
\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}
, which, at this point, behave like
\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sigma}\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}
, and thus not yield the sharp estimate (or even an improved estimate) that we will need to control borderline terms.

4.2.
Strong low order
C_{G}
-norm estimates

Now, we can prove the main supremum norm estimates in this section:

Lemma 4.3

(Strong low order
C_{G}
-norm estimates)
.

The following estimates hold:

(4.4a)

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{C^{13}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,

(4.4b)

\displaystyle\|\Sigma\|_{C^{12}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,

(4.4c)

\displaystyle\|G-\gamma\|_{C^{12}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,

(4.4d)

\displaystyle\|G^{-1}-\gamma^{-1}\|_{C^{12}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,

(4.4e)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\phi\|_{C^{12}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sqrt{\varepsilon}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

(4.4f)

\displaystyle\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\|_{C^{10}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sqrt{\varepsilon}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

(4.4g)

\displaystyle\|\bm{B}\|_{C^{11}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a^{2-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(4.4h)

\displaystyle\|\bm{E}\|_{C^{12}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

Proof.

Before going into the individual estimates, we collect the following commutator term estimates from the expressions in (
11.10a
)-(
11.10b
):

(4.5)

\displaystyle\|[\partial_{t},\nabla^{J}]\zeta\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,a^{-3}\|N+1\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\|\Sigma\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\|\zeta\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}+\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\|N\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\|\zeta\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\|[\partial_{t},\nabla^{J}]\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,a^{-3}\|N+1\|_{C^{J}_{G}}\left(\|\nabla^{J}\Sigma\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{0}_{G}}+\|\Sigma\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\right)+\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\|N\|_{C^{J}_{G}}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}

With this in hand, we will prove each estimate by iterating over the derivative order as long as the bootstrap assumptions can be applied. In each step, we use the previously obtained estimates at lower order to control the commutator term (with some additional care for
\mathfrak{T}=\Sigma
which we need to consider first), while we can use similar arguments to those at order
0
to control the
“core”
of the evolution equations.

To start out, we apply (
4.2b
) on
\Sigma
and the bootstrap assumption (
3.17h
) on
N
to the rescaled evolution equations (
2.28a
)-(
2.28b
) and deduce

(4.7)

\lvert\partial_{t}G^{\pm 1}\rvert_{G}=\lvert\partial_{t}(G^{\pm 1}-\gamma^{\pm 1})\rvert_{G}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3}+\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3}\,.

(
4.4b
):
We assume

(4.8)

\|\Sigma\|_{{C}^{J-1}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

to be satisfied for some
J\in\{1,\dots,12\}
(For
J=1
, this is true by (
4.2b
)). Observe the following:

\partial_{t}\lvert\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}=2\langle\partial_{t}\nabla^{J}\Sigma,\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rangle_{G}+\partial_{t}G^{-1}\ast\nabla^{J}\Sigma\ast\nabla^{J}\Sigma

Now, we commute (
2.28d
) with
\nabla^{J}
: As before,
\nabla^{J}\partial_{t}\Sigma
is bounded by
\varepsilon a^{-c\sigma}
for any admissible
J
. Hence and using (
4.7
),

\partial_{t}\lvert\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3}\lvert\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}+\left(\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}+\|[\partial_{t},\nabla^{J}]\Sigma\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\right)\lvert\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rvert_{G}

is satisfied. Looking at the commutator term using (
4.6
), we have with (
4.2b
) that

\|[\partial_{t},\nabla^{J}]\Sigma\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3}\|\Sigma\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{G}}+a^{-3}\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}^{2}+\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}\|\Sigma\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\,.

Altogether, we obtain

\partial_{t}\lvert\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rvert_{G}^{2}\lesssim\left(\varepsilon a^{-3}\|\Sigma\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{G}}+\varepsilon a^{-c\sigma}+\varepsilon^{2}a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\lvert\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rvert_{G}\,.

With Lemma
11.2
as well as the initial data assumption (
3.10
) and the integral formula (
2.6
) with
p=c\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, this implies

\lvert\nabla^{J}\Sigma\rvert_{G}(t)\lesssim\,\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon a^{-3}\|\Sigma\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{G}(\Sigma_{s})}\,ds+\varepsilon\left(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)

and consequently, after taking the supremum on the left and applying the Gronwall lemma,

\|\Sigma\|_{\dot{C}^{J}_{G}(\Sigma_{s})}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,.

Combining this with (
4.8
) proves the statement up to order
J
, and hence shows (
4.4b
) by iterating the argument up to
J=12
.

(
4.4a
):
We again assume that

(4.9)

\|\Psi\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

holds for
J\in\{1,2,\dots,13\}
. Observe that

\lvert\partial_{t}\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}\lesssim a\|N+1\|_{C^{J+1}_{G}}\|\nabla\phi\|_{C^{J+1}_{G}}+\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\|\nabla N\|_{C^{J}_{G}}(1+\|\Psi\|_{C^{J}_{G}})+\|[\partial_{t},\nabla^{J}]\Psi\|_{C^{0}_{G}}

By (
3.17h
), (
3.17b
) and (
3.17a
), the first two summands can be bounded (up to constant) by
\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}
. By (
4.9
), (
4.4b
) and (
3.17h
) and using (
4.5
), the commutator term is bounded (up to constant) by
\varepsilon^{2}a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
. Altogether,

\lvert\partial_{t}\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}+\varepsilon^{2}a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

follows. Inserting this and (
4.7
) into

\lvert\partial_{t}\left(\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}^{2}\right)\rvert\leq\lvert\partial_{t}G^{-1}\rvert_{G}\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}^{2}+2\lvert\partial_{t}\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}\cdot\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}

implies, with Lemma
11.2
,

\displaystyle\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}(t)\leq

\displaystyle\,\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert(t_{0})+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\lvert\partial_{t}G^{-1}\rvert\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}+\lvert\partial_{t}\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}\right)(s)\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{2}+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\left(\varepsilon a(s)^{-3}\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi(s,\cdot)\rvert_{G}+\varepsilon a(s)^{1-c\sigma}+\varepsilon^{2}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\,ds\,.

We obtain using (
2.6
):

\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{G}(t)\lesssim\varepsilon a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon a(s)^{-3}\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi(s,\cdot)\rvert_{G}\,ds

The Gronwall lemma, applying (
2.5
) and taking the supremum over
\Sigma_{t}
then implies
\lvert\nabla^{J}\Psi\rvert_{\dot{C}^{J}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
. This proves (
4.2a
) by iterating over
J
and adding up the individual seminorms.

(
4.4c
)-(
4.4d
):
Note that (
4.7
) implies (
4.4c
) at order
0
since one has

\lvert\partial_{t}(\lvert G-\gamma\rvert_{G})^{2}\rvert\lesssim\lvert\partial_{t}G^{-1}\rvert_{G}\lvert G-\gamma\rvert_{G}^{2}+\lvert\partial_{t}(G-\gamma)\rvert_{G}\lvert G-\gamma\rvert_{G}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3}(1+\lvert G-\gamma\rvert_{G})\lvert G-\gamma\rvert_{G}

which we can apply the Gronwall lemma to after integrating, along with (
2.7
) for the error term, as in the proof of (
4.4b
).

For higher orders, commuting (
2.28a
) with
\nabla^{J}
and inserting (
4.4b
) and (
3.17h
) implies

\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{J}(G-\gamma)\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}+\|[\partial_{t},\nabla^{J}](G-\gamma)\|_{C^{0}_{G}}

with

\|[\partial_{t},\nabla^{J}](G-\gamma)\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim\left(\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma}\right)\|G-\gamma\|_{C^{J-1}_{G}}\,.

Once again doing the same iterative argument over
J\leq 12
and assuming the estimate to hold up to
J-1
, this altogether becomes

\|\partial_{t}\nabla^{J}(G-\gamma)\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}},

implying with (
2.6
)

\|\nabla^{J}(G-\gamma)\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}+\varepsilon\int_{t}^{t_{0}}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,ds\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,.

The argument for
G^{-1}-\gamma^{-1}
is completely analogous.

(
4.4e
):
We only prove the statement for
C^{0}_{G}
, the full estimate extends from there by the same iterative arguments as above. Considering (
2.32b
), Lemma
4.1
, (
4.2a
) and (
2.3
), we have

\lvert\partial_{t}\nabla\phi\rvert_{G}\lesssim a^{-3}\left(\lvert\nabla\Psi\rvert_{G}+\lvert\nabla N\rvert_{G}\right)

and thus, with (
4.4a
) and the bootstrap assumption (
3.17h
),

\lvert\partial_{t}\nabla\phi\rvert_{G}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,.

With (
4.7
), this implies

\lvert\partial_{t}\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert_{G}\rvert^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{-3}\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert_{G}^{2}+\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert_{G}

and the statement follows as usual by applying Lemma
11.2
, (
2.7
) and the Gronwall lemma.

(
4.4f
):
This follows as in the proof of (
4.4c
) using (
2.33
) and (
2.28a
) and their commuted analogues.

Once again, for
C^{0}_{G}
, we have

(
4.4g
):
This is obtained immediately from commuting (
2.29d
) with
\nabla^{J}
and applying (
4.4b
). Notice that the Levi-Civita tensor can be absorbed into the implicit constants since
\lvert\varepsilon[G]\rvert=\sqrt{6}
holds (see (
11.2c
)).

(
4.4h
):
This follows like in the proof of (
4.2c
) from applying (
3.17f
), (
3.17b
) and (
4.4b
) to the constraint equation (
2.29c
) commuted with
\nabla^{J}
.

∎

4.3.
Other useful a priori observations

Before moving on to the energy estimates, we collect a differentiation identity and lay the groundwork for energy coercivity:

Lemma 4.4

(The volume form and differentiation of integrals)
.

Let
\mu_{G}=\sqrt{\det G}
denote the volume element with regards to
G
. It satisfies

(4.10)

\partial_{t}\mu_{G}=\frac{1}{2}\mu_{G}(G^{-1})^{ij}\partial_{t}G_{ij}=-N\tau\mu_{G}\,\,,

and hence one has

(4.11)

\|\mu_{G}-\mu_{\gamma}\|_{C^{0}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon\,.

on
(\Sigma_{t})_{t\in(t_{Boot},t_{0}]}
. Further, for any differentiable function
\zeta
, one has

(4.12)

\partial_{t}\int_{M}\zeta{\text{{vol}}}_{G}=\int_{M}\partial_{t}\zeta{\text{{vol}}}_{G}-\int_{M}N\tau\cdot\zeta{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

Proof.

From (
2.28a
), we obtain
(G^{-1})^{ij}\partial_{t}G_{ij}=-2N\tau
,
and (
4.10
) follows by

\partial_{t}\mu_{G}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\det G}(G^{-1})^{ij}\partial_{t}G_{ij}=-N\tau\mu_{G}\,.

Hence, we have using (
3.17h
) and the initial data estimate (
3.14
) that

\displaystyle\lvert\mu_{G}-\mu_{\gamma}\rvert(t,\cdot)\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon a(s)^{1-c\sigma}\lvert\mu_{G}-\mu_{\gamma}\rvert(s.\cdot)\,ds

holds, and thus (
4.11
) after applying the Gronwall lemma.

Finally, we obtain (
4.12
) by writing
{\text{{vol}}}_{G}=\frac{\mu_{G}}{\mu_{\gamma}}{\text{{vol}}}_{\gamma}
and inserting (
4.10
)
∎

Lemma 4.5

(Preliminary Sobolev norm estimates)
.

Let
\zeta
be a scalar function and
\mathfrak{T}
be a symmetric
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent
(0,2)
-tensor, and let
l\in\{1,\dots,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}9\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}
. Then, on
(t_{Boot},t_{0}]
, the following estimates are satisfied: For
l>5
, one has:

(4.13a)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\Delta\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}

(4.13b)

\displaystyle\|\zeta\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\Delta^{l}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\Delta^{m}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|\zeta\|_{C_{G}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-12\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-3)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)

(4.13c)

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{2l+1}\|\zeta\|_{\dot{H}^{m}_{G}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\nabla\Delta^{l}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\nabla\Delta^{m}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+\|\nabla\zeta\|_{C_{G}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-12\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-2)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right)

(4.13d)

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{2l+1}\|\nabla\zeta\|_{\dot{H}^{m}_{G}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\nabla^{2}\Delta^{l}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\nabla^{2}\Delta^{m}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+\|\nabla\zeta\|_{C_{G}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-11\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-1)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right)

and

(4.14a)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\Delta^{l}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\Delta^{m}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C_{G}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-11\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-2)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right)

(4.14b)

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{2l+1}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{m}_{G}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\nabla\Delta^{l}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\nabla\Delta^{m}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C_{G}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-10\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-1)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right)

More precisely, the Ricci energy terms can be dropped in all of the above estimates for
l\leq 5
.

Remark 4.6
.

We stress that Lemma
4.5
is crucial for everything that follows in multiple ways:

Firstly, the
L^{2}_{G}
-norms containing
\zeta
and
\mathfrak{T}
on the right hand sides above except (
4.13d
) are in precisely the form the energies in Definition
3.9
take. Hence, this is what will actually yield near-coercivity of our energies since the
C_{G}
-norms can be controlled by
a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
or better using the a priori estimates from Lemma
4.3
, as well as (
3.17h
) for the lapse. This will be shown more explicitly as an intermediary step in improving the bootstrap assumptions for
\mathcal{C}
(see proof of Corollary
7.3
).

Secondly, a downside of using
\Delta
as the main differential operator to commute with the Einstein scalar-field system is that it creates error terms that we can only bound by Sobolev norms and not directly express as energies. Thus, we need a way to translate this information back to energies to formulate energy inequalities. A lot of this is done
“under the hood”
in the error term estimates in subsection
11.4
.

Finally, some top order terms also do not appear in a way that their
L^{2}
-norm is directly the square root of an energy (see, for example, the term
a\nabla^{2}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N
in (
2.35
)), and some borderline terms would lead to nonintegrable divergences if we were to incur additional divergences in estimation (see, for example, the first term in (
11.14a
)). Lemma
4.5
precisely provides a way to relate these terms to energies . Additionally, by applying these estimates for terms of the form
\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\zeta
and
\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\mathfrak{T}
, one can avoid high order curvature energies that run risk of breaking the energy hierarchy.

Proof.

Since the arguments for all of the inequalities above are very similar, we only prove (
4.14a
) in full and then briefly adress the other estimates.

Denoting
\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{2l}k_{1}k_{2}}=\nabla_{i_{1}}\dots\nabla_{i_{2l}}\mathfrak{T}_{k_{1}k_{2}}\,
, we compute with the commutator formula (
11.6c
) and strong
C_{G}
-norm estimate (
4.4f
):

\displaystyle\int_{M}\lvert\nabla^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}=

\displaystyle\,-\int_{M}\langle\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{T}},\Delta\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rangle_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\langle\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{T}},\nabla\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rangle_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}+\int_{M}\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\ast\left[\nabla\text{{Ric}}[G]\ast\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}+\text{{Ric}}[G]\ast\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\right]\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{M}\lvert\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}+(1+\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+2G\|_{{C}^{1}_{G}})\cdot\left[\int_{M}\lvert\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}+\int_{M}\lvert\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{M}\lvert\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\int_{M}\lvert\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

In the final step, we used integration by parts to obtain

a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\int_{M}\lvert\nabla\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\leq\int_{M}\lvert\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}\cdot a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\lvert\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\lesssim\int_{M}\left(\lvert\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}+a^{-2c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\lvert\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}\right)\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

and updated
c
. This already shows (
4.14a
) for
l=1
. Assume now that (
4.14a
) holds up to some
l\in\mathbb{N},\,l\leq 9
and
any
symmetric
\Sigma_{t}
-tangent
(0,2)
-tensor field. By applying (
11.6d
), we have

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}=

\displaystyle\,\nabla^{2l}\Delta\mathfrak{T}+[\Delta,\nabla^{2}]\nabla^{2l-2}\mathfrak{T}+\dots+\nabla^{2l-2}[\Delta,\nabla^{2}]\mathfrak{T}

(4.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\nabla^{2l}\Delta\mathfrak{T}+\sum_{I_{\text{{Ric}}}+I_{\mathfrak{T}}=2l}\nabla^{I_{\text{{Ric}}}}(\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G)\ast\nabla^{I_{\mathfrak{T}}}\mathfrak{T}+G\ast\nabla^{2l}\mathfrak{T}

Subsequently, we have for
l>5
using the strong
C_{G}
-norm estimate (
4.4f
) for any Ricci term of order 10 or lower that

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{2(l+1)}_{G}}^{2}=\int_{M}\lvert\nabla^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\lesssim\|\Delta\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{2l}_{G}}^{2}+\left(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}+\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C_{G}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-11\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}^{2}\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+\frac{2}{9}G\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}\,,

and get the same estimate without the final term for
l\leq 5.
By assumption, we can estimate
\|\Delta\mathfrak{T}\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}
,
\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}
and
\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+2G\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}
as in (
4.14a
), and get the following for
l>5
:

\displaystyle\int_{M}\lvert\nabla^{2l+2}\mathfrak{T}\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\left[\|\Delta^{l}\Delta\mathfrak{T}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{G}}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\Delta^{m+1}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\Delta\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-12\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}_{G}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-2)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right]

\displaystyle\,+\left[a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\Delta^{l}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\Delta^{m}\mathfrak{T}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{G}}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-12\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}_{G}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-2)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right]

\displaystyle\,+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-11\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}_{G}}^{2}\left[\mathcal{E}^{(2l)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)+\left(\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-2)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)+\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-2)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\Delta^{l+1}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{l}\|\Delta^{m}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{C^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2l-10{}_{G}}}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\right)

For
l=5
, we get analogous estimates dropping the Ricci energies in the first two lines, and for
l=4
, the same with all curvature terms dropped.

To prove the statement for
l+1
, it now remains to be shown that
\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\dot{H}^{2l+1}_{G}}^{2}
can be bounded by the same right hand side (up to constant) as above. By integration by parts, one has

\|\nabla^{2l+1}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}\lesssim\|\nabla^{2l}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}+\|\Delta\nabla^{2l}\mathfrak{T}\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2},

where the latter tensor is precisely
\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}
which we just treated, and the former is covered by the induction assumption at order
2l
. So, (
4.14a
) now follows for
l+1
, and thus by iteration up to
l=10
.

The proof of (
4.14b
) is analogous – we note that since we actually only needed a strong estimate on
\|\text{{Ric}}[G]+2G\|_{C^{9}_{G}}
for the previous inequality, but (
4.4f
) holds at
C^{10}_{G}
, this gives enough room to extend the argument in full despite the extra derivative order.

For both, note that we only need to estimate the Ricci terms in the
L^{2}_{G}
-norm if one can’t apply the a priori estimate (
4.4f
) to all
\nabla^{I_{\text{{Ric}}}}\text{{Ric}}[G]
that occur in (
4.15
), and thus we could easily adjust the proof such that the Ricci energy does not occur in any of the proofs as long as
2l-1\leq 10
is satisfied, so for
l\leq 5
.

The estimates (
4.13b
)-(
4.13d
) are proved identically, the only difference being that one order of curvature less enters in the commutator terms in (
4.15
), leading to one order less in curvature in total. For (
4.13a
), we note that we can avoid incurring any
L^{2}
-norm by carefully repeating the argument we made for
\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}
using (
11.6a
):

\displaystyle\int_{M}\lvert\nabla^{2}\zeta\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}=

\displaystyle\,\int_{M}-\langle\nabla\zeta,\nabla\Delta\zeta\rangle_{G}+\int_{M}\text{{Ric}}[G]\ast\nabla\zeta\ast\nabla\zeta\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\int_{M}\lvert\Delta\zeta\rvert_{G}^{2}+a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\int_{M}\lvert\nabla\zeta\rvert_{G}^{2}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

∎

5.
Big Bang stability: Elliptic lapse estimates

In this section, we study the elliptic structure of the equations (
2.30a
)-(
2.30b
), which admit estimates controlling (time-scaled) lapse energies by other energy quantities. To this end, we recast these equations as follows:

Definition 5.1

(Elliptic operators)
.

For any (sufficiently regular) scalar function
\zeta
on
\Sigma_{t}
, we define the following differential operators:

(5.1a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\zeta=

\displaystyle\,a^{4}\Delta\zeta-f\cdot\zeta,

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{3}a^{4}+12\pi C^{2}+\underbrace{\langle\Sigma,\Sigma\rangle_{G}+8\pi\Psi^{2}+16\pi C\Psi}_{=:F}

(5.1b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}\zeta=

\displaystyle\,a^{4}\Delta\zeta-\tilde{f}\cdot\zeta,

\displaystyle\quad\tilde{f}=\frac{1}{3}a^{4}+12\pi C^{2}+\underbrace{a^{4}\left[R[G]+\frac{2}{3}-8\pi\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert^{2}_{G}\right]}_{=\tilde{F}}

Note that the lapse equations (
2.30a
), respectively (
2.30b
), now read

(5.2)

\mathcal{L}N=F,\ \text{respectively}\ \tilde{\mathcal{L}}N=\tilde{F}.\,

Furthermore, observe that

(5.3a)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L},\Delta]\zeta=

\displaystyle\,\Delta f\cdot\zeta+2\langle\nabla f,\nabla\zeta\rangle_{G}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta F\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot\zeta+2\langle\nabla F,\nabla\zeta\rangle_{G}\,,

(5.3b)

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathcal{L}},\Delta]\zeta=

\displaystyle\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\tilde{F}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot\zeta+2\langle\nabla\tilde{F},\nabla\zeta\rangle_{G}\,.

5.1.
Elliptic lapse estimates with
\mathcal{L}

We first study the elliptic operator
\mathcal{L}
, which will admit weak lapse energy estimates in terms of scalar field quantities and
\Sigma
, up to curvature errors, that can in particular be utilized at high orders without having to resort to higher derivative levels. Before moving on to the estimates themselves, we collect a couple of inequalities we can deduce from the bootstrap assumptions and strong
C_{G}
-norm estimates.

Remark 5.2
.

There exists a constant
K>0
such that, for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, the following estimates hold:

•

F\geq-K\varepsilon
, and equivalently
f\geq 12\pi C^{2}-K\varepsilon
. This is ensured by (
4.2b
) and (
4.2a
). In particular, we can assume
\varepsilon
to have been small enough such that
f-6\pi C^{2}
can be bounded from below by a positive constant that is independent of
\varepsilon
(for example
3\pi C^{2}
).

•

\lvert\nabla f\rvert_{G}=\lvert\nabla F\rvert_{G}\leq K\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
. This is given by (
4.4b
) and (
4.4a
).

Lemma 5.3

(Elliptic estimates with
\mathcal{L}
)
.

Consider scalar functions
\zeta,Z
on
\Sigma_{t}
that satisfy

(5.4)

\mathcal{L}\zeta=Z\,.

Then,

(5.5)

a^{4}\|\Delta\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+a^{2}\|\nabla\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\|\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}\lesssim\|Z\|_{L^{2}_{G}}

Proof.

The proof follows along the same lines as that of
[
Spe18
, Lemma 16.5]
: First, we obtain the following by multiplying (
5.4
) with
-\zeta
and integrating:
and using that
f-6\pi C^{2}
is bounded from below by a positive constant (see the first point in Remark
5.2
):

\int_{M}(a^{4}\lvert\nabla\zeta\rvert_{G}^{2}+\lvert\zeta\rvert^{2}){\text{{vol}}}_{G}\lesssim\|Z\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{G}}

Next, we multiply (
5.4
) with
a^{4}\Delta\zeta
and obtain

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(a^{8}\lvert\Delta\zeta\rvert^{2}+a^{4}\lvert\nabla\zeta\rvert_{G}^{2}f\right)\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\lvert Z\rvert^{2}+\frac{a^{8}}{2}\lvert\Delta\zeta\rvert^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{2}\lvert\zeta\rvert^{2}+\frac{a^{4}}{2}\lvert\nabla\zeta\rvert_{G}^{2}\right)a^{2}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}_{G}}\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

Using the second point in Remark
5.2
as well as the previous step, we can now conclude

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(a^{8}\lvert\Delta\zeta\rvert^{2}+a^{4}\lvert\nabla\zeta\rvert_{G}^{2}\right)\,{\text{{vol}}}_{G}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\varepsilon)\|Z\|_{L^{2}_{G}}^{2}

and thus the statement after rearranging.
∎

Corollary 5.4

(Intermediary elliptic lapse estimate with
\mathcal{L}
)
.

The following estimates hold for any
l\in\{0,\dots,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}10\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}
:

\displaystyle a^{4}\|\Delta^{l+1}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+a^{2}\|\nabla\Delta^{l}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\|\Delta^{l}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\Delta^{l}F\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|F\|_{H^{2(l-1)}_{G}}}_{\text{not present for }l=0}+\underbrace{\varepsilon^{2}a^{4-c\sigma}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-4)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)}}_{\text{not present for }l\leq 1}

Proof.

We prove the statement by induction over
l\in\mathbb{N}
: For
l=0
, the estimates immediately follow from (
5.2
) and Lemma
5.3
. Assume the statement to be satisfied up to
l-1
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,l\leq 11
. We get applying (
5.3a
) iteratively:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Delta^{l}N=

\displaystyle\sum_{I=1}^{2l-1}\nabla^{I}F\ast\nabla^{2l-I}N+(N+1)\Delta^{l}F

Applying Lemma
5.3
to
\zeta=\Delta^{l}N
as well as Lemma
4.1
yields

a^{4}\|\Delta^{l+1}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+a^{2}\|\nabla\Delta^{l}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\|\Delta^{l}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}\lesssim\sum_{I=1}^{2l-1}\|\nabla^{I}F\ast\nabla^{2l-I}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\|\Delta^{l}F\|_{L^{2}_{G}}

Hence, using (
4.13c
) (replacing
l
with
l-1
) and (
3.17h
), (
4.4a
) and (
4.4b
) to estimate low order terms, we get

\displaystyle a^{4}\|\Delta^{l+1}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+a^{2}\|\nabla\Delta^{l}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\|\Delta^{l}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|\Delta^{l}F\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}\|\nabla\Delta^{m}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\varepsilon a^{4-c\sigma}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-4)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)}\right)

\displaystyle\,+\varepsilon a^{4-c\sigma}\left(\|F\|_{H^{2(l-1)}_{G}}+\|\nabla\Delta^{l-1}F\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-4)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)}\right)

For the top order lapse term, we can redistribute the divergent prefactor as follows:

\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla\Delta^{l-1}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon\|\Delta^{l}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\varepsilon a^{-2c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\Delta^{l-1}N\|_{L^{2}_{G}}

The lower order lapse terms as well as
\|\nabla\Delta^{l-1}F\|_{L^{2}_{G}}
can be estimated similarly, just without having to redistribute the prefactor. Updating
c>0
and rearranging then yields the statement at order
l
for suitably small
\varepsilon>0
, and thus the entire statement after iteration.
∎

Corollary 5.5

(Lapse energy estimates with
\mathcal{L}
)
.

For any
l\in\{0,\dots,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}9\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}
, one has

\displaystyle\,a^{8}\mathcal{E}^{(2(l+1))}(N,\cdot)+a^{4}\mathcal{E}^{(2l+1)}(N,\cdot)+\mathcal{E}^{(2l)}(N,\cdot)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(2l)}(\Sigma,\cdot)+\mathcal{E}^{(2l)}(\phi,\cdot)+\underbrace{\varepsilon^{2}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left[\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2(l-1))}(\Sigma,\cdot)+\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2(l-1))}(\phi,\cdot)\right]}_{\text{not present for }l=0}

\displaystyle\,+\underbrace{\left(\varepsilon^{4}a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{2}a^{8-c\sigma}\right)\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l-3)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)}_{\text{not present for }l\leq 1}

Proof.

Note that, by Corollary
5.4
, all that needs to be done is to relate all Sobolev norms of
F
that occur to the respective energies. Schematically, we have

\Delta^{l}F=16\pi(\Delta^{l}\Psi)(\Psi+C)+2\langle\Delta^{l}\Sigma,\Sigma\rangle_{G}+\sum_{I=1}^{2l-1}\left(\nabla^{I}\Psi\ast\nabla^{2l-I}\Psi+\nabla^{I}\Sigma\ast\nabla^{2l-I}\Sigma\right)\,.

For the first two terms, we can use (
4.2a
) and (
4.2b
) to bound
\lvert\Sigma\rvert_{G}
and
\lvert\Psi+C\rvert
by
\varepsilon
and
1
up to constant, respectively. For the remaining terms, we similarly always bound the lower order in
L^{\infty}_{G}
with (
4.4a
)-(
4.4b
) and bound the higher order with the energy estimates in Lemma
4.5
. Further, we can use (
3.8
) to redistribute divergent prefactors onto energies of order
l-2
and lower. This already incurs the terms on the right hand side of the claimed estimate, and the lower order norms of
F
only incur at equivalent or weaker error terms.
∎

5.2.
Elliptic lapse estimates with
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}

While the estimates in the previous subsection are useful at high orders, they aren’t enough to close the bootstrap assumptions for
N
. This can be achieved by deriving estimates in terms of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
– however, due to the explicit presence of Ricci terms in this version of the lapse equation, we use this to bound
N
at lower orders. Since the arguments are largely identical to the ones above, we only sketch the proofs.

Remark 5.6
.

Note that, when replacing
f
by
\tilde{f}
and
F
by
\tilde{F}
in Remark
5.2
, the same statements hold for a suitable constant
K
. In fact, the bootstrap assumptions on
\text{{Ric}}[G]
and
\nabla\phi
even imply
\|\tilde{F}\|_{C^{1}_{G}}\lesssim\varepsilon a^{4-c\sigma}
, noting
\lvert R[G]+\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}\rvert_{G}\leq\lvert G^{-1}\rvert_{G}\lvert\text{{Ric}}[G]+\nicefrac{{2}}{{9}}G\rvert_{G}\lesssim\lvert\text{{Ric}}[G]+\nicefrac{{2}}{{9}}G\rvert_{G}
and
\lvert\nabla R[G]\rvert_{G}=\lvert\nabla(R[G]+\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})\rvert_{G}\lesssim\lvert\text{{Ric}}[G]+\nicefrac{{2}}{{9}}G\rvert_{G}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Any scalar functions
\zeta
and
Z
such that

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}\zeta=Z

holds satisfy the estimate

\displaystyle a^{4}\|\Delta\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+a^{2}\|\nabla\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}+\|\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{G}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|Z\|_{L^{2}_{G}}\,.

Proof.

The proof follows identically to Lemma
5.3
since all tools relating to
f
and
F
used in proving these statements were collected in Remark
5.2
, and these extend to
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
by Remark
5.6
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

For
l\in\{0,\dots,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}8\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle a^{8}\mathcal{E}^{(2(l+1))}(N,\cdot)+a^{4}\mathcal{E}^{(2l+1)}(N,\cdot)+\mathcal{E}^{(2l)}(N,\cdot)\lesssim

\displaystyle\,a^{8}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq 2l)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)+{\varepsilon}a^{8-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\|\nabla\phi\|_{H^{2l}_{G}}^{2}

Proof.

As in the proof of Corollary
5.4
, this follows by commuting
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
with
\Delta^{l}
iteratively and applying (
4.4e
) and (
4.4f
) to bound lower order terms within the nonlinearities.
∎

6.
Big Bang stability: Energy and norm estimates

In this section, we derive energy estimates for matter variables and the geometric quantities as well as Sobolev norm estimates for spatial derivatives of
\phi
and for metric quantities. To derive all of the inequalities in this section beside the elliptic inequality in Lemma
6.10
and the bound on
\nabla\phi
in Lemma
6.5
, we will use the same basic strategy. Hence, we give a brief overview on the form our integral inequalities are going to take and how we intend to obtain improved energy bounds from there:

Remark 6.1

(Integral inequalities and the Gronwall argument)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{L}
denote an energy or a squared Sobolev(-type) norm at derivative level
L\in 2\mathbb{N}
, for example
\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\phi,\cdot)
. To derive an integral inequality for
\mathcal{F}_{L}
, we will take its time derivative, apply the respective commuted evolution equations in the integrand, estimate the resulting terms and integrate that inequality. Schematically, the resulting integral inequalities for
\mathcal{F}_{L}
then take the following form:

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{F}_{L}(t)+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\langle\text{ultimately nonnegative contributions}\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{F}_{L}(t_{0})+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}a(s)^{-3}+a(s)^{-1-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{L}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle\,+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}a(s)^{-3}\langle\text{other energies/squared Sobolev norms at same derivative level}\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle\,+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\langle\text{energies/squared Sobolev norms at derivative levels up to }L-2\rangle\,ds

For some inequalities, we won’t be able to derive any benefitial
\varepsilon
-prefactors in the penultimate line. For example, for
\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\Sigma,\cdot)
, linear lapse terms in the evolution of
\Sigma
incur a term of the form

\int_{t}^{t_{0}}a(s)^{-3}\cdot a(s)^{4}\|\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}N\|_{\dot{H}^{2}_{G}}\cdot\sqrt{\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\Sigma,s)}\,ds

on the right hand side, which after applying lapse energy estimates creates
\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\phi,\cdot)
on the right. However, combining the respective inequalities for the core energy mechanism at each derivative level with appropriate scaling, this will then combine to an inequality of the following form for a total energy, which we informally denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}
:

(6.1)

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}(t)+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\langle\text{nonnegative quantity}\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}(t_{0})+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}a(s)^{-3}+a(s)^{-1-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle\,+\underbrace{\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}a(s)^{-3-c\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}\langle\text{already improved terms}\rangle\,ds}_{\text{not present for }L=0}

\displaystyle\,+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}(t)+\underbrace{\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot\langle\text{small lower order terms}\rangle(t)}_{\text{not present for }L=0}

In the mentioned example, scaling
\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\Sigma,\cdot)
by
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}
turns the offending term int
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}a(s)^{-3}\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\phi,s)
, which can be absorbed into the first line.

Applying the Gronwall lemma (see Lemma
11.1
) and the initial data assumption (which implies
\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}
) then yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}(t)\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\biggr(\varepsilon^{4}+\underbrace{\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}a(s)^{-3-c\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}\langle\text{already improved terms}\rangle\,ds}_{\text{not present for }L=0}\biggr)

\displaystyle\,\cdot\exp\left(K\cdot\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}a(s)^{-3}+a(s)^{-1-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,ds\right)

\displaystyle\,+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal{F}_{total,L}(t)+\underbrace{\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot\langle\text{small lower order terms}\rangle(t)}_{\text{not present for }L=0}

for some constant
K>0
. By (
2.7
) and (
2.6
), the exponential factor can be bounded by
a^{-c\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}
, up to constant and updating
c>0
. Furthermore,
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal{F}_{total,L}(t)
in the final line can be absorbed by the left hand side after updating the implicit constant in
“
\lesssim
”
.

Hence, for
L=0
, this implies
\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},0}\lesssim\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon^{4}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a^{-c\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}
, and thus leads to improved bounds for base level energy quantities (see Remark
3.19
for the precise scaling hierarchy that will achieve).
By iterating this argument for
L>0
, the already improved terms will then be bounded (at worst) by
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon^{4}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a^{-c\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}
, and (
2.6
) shows that the first line can be bounded by
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon^{4}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a^{-c\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}
after updating
c
. This allows us to bound
\mathcal{F}_{\text{total},L}
by
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon^{4}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a^{-c\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{8}}}
for any
L
up to and including top order.

Finally, we mention that, to control energies at order
L
, we need to consider scaled energies at order
L+1
within
\mathcal{F}_{total,L}
– this arises since the scalar field occurs at first order in the evolution equations for
\bm{E}
and
\bm{B}
. We avoid losing derivatives by employing the div-curl-estimate in Lemma
6.10
at order
L+1
, which allows us to control
a^{4}\mathcal{E}^{(L+1)}(\Sigma,\cdot)
by quantities at order
L
. This is precisely what generates the non-integral terms in the schematics above. We note that it is crucial that the scalar field occurs at no worse scaling than
a^{-1}
in (
2.38a
)-(
2.38b
) – else, moving to these time-scaled estimates at order
L+1
would lose too many powers of
a
and lead to exponentially divergent terms after applying the Gronwall argument.

Recall that
L^{2}_{G}
-norm estimates for error terms arising in the Laplace-commuted equations in Lemma
2.11
are collected in Section
11.4
. Low order estimates (in particular estimates for
L=2
) could often be improved if needed by more carefully avoiding curvature error terms, but we refrain from doing so where it is not necessary to keep estimates as unified as possible.

6.1.
Integral and energy estimates for the scalar field

6.1.1.
Scalar field energy estimates

Over the following two lemmas, we prove the core energy estimates to control the matter variables, which are immediately prepared differently at base, intermediate and top order for the total energy estimates in Section
7
.

Lemma 6.2
.

[Even order scalar field energy estimates] Let
t\in(t_{Boot},t_{0}]
. Then, one has

(6.2)

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{E}^{(0)}(\phi,t)+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\dot{a}(s)a(s)^{3}\mathcal{E}^{(1)}(N,s)+\frac{\dot{a}(s)}{a(s)}\mathcal{E}^{(0)}(N,s)\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{E}^{(0)}(\phi,t_{0})+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon a(s)^{-3}\mathcal{E}^{(0)}(\phi,s)+\varepsilon a^{-3}\mathcal{E}^{(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}(\Sigma,s)\,ds\,.

Further, for any
L\in 2\mathbb{N},\,2\leq L\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}18\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, the following estimate is satisfied:

(6.3)

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\phi,t)+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\dot{a}(s)a(s)^{3}\mathcal{E}^{(L+1)}(N,s)+\frac{\dot{a}(s)}{a(s)}\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(N,s)\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\phi,t_{0})+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a(s)^{-3}+a(s)^{-1-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\phi,s)\,ds

\displaystyle\,+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon a(s)^{-3}\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\Sigma,s)+\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}a(s)^{-3}\mathcal{E}^{(L-2)}(\text{{Ric}},\cdot)\,ds

\displaystyle\,+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq L-2)}(\phi,s)+\varepsilon a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq L-2)}(\Sigma,s)\,ds

\displaystyle\underbrace{+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}^{(\leq L-4)}(\text{{Ric}},s)\,ds}_{\text{if }L\geq 4}

Remark 6.3
.

This proof relies on two mechanisms: Firstly, we use the structure of the wave equation and integration by parts to cancel the highest order scalar field derivative terms. Getting this cancellation is what necessitates scaling the potential term in the scalar field energy by
a^{4}
. Secondly, we deal with the highest order lapse terms using the elliptic structure of the (Laplace-commuted) lapse equation – both in an indirect way by invoking the elliptic energy estimate in Corollary
5.5
as well as by directly inserting (
2.37a
) to cancel some ill-behaved terms. While the framework significantly differs from the scalar field energy estimates
[
Spe18
]
, these two core mechanisms also appear there and play similarly crucial roles.

Proof.

Since the arguments are essentially the same, we will only write down the proof for
L\geq 2
in full and make short comments throughout the argument which terms do not occur for
L=0
.

We use the evolution equations (
2.39a
) and (
2.39b
) and Lemma
4.4
to compute for
L\geq 2
:

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}^{(L)}(\phi,\cdot)=\int_{M}

\displaystyle\,-2\partial_{t}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi\cdot\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\Psi-2a^{4}\langle\partial_{t}\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi,\nabla\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi\rangle_{G}-a^{4}(\partial_{t}G^{-1})^{ij}\nabla_{i}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi\nabla_{j}\Delta^{\frac{L}{2}}\phi
